These 7,128 sites fall under types with no folds row and no band membership, meaning they're new since the freeze—likely because the sweep reshuffled site membership and union-find clusters, generating fresh hash names. I need to decide how to classify them: either treat them as drift and fold them in by checking for E1 evidence of canonical bodies, or try mapping them back to old band/tail names using site overlap with the previous census.

Applying the planner's own rule that "the tail = every TNC map type not in the band" would mechanically classify these as tail by definition. But this feels too convenient—these could actually be drifted band types whose sites split off, and labeling them as below-band could mask sites that deserve band status. The scale is suspicious too: 7,128 sites is close to the entire tail's size, and one type alone (e5341003) has 768 sites, which doesn't look like typical below-band magnitude.

So I'm thinking the honest approach is to treat these as post-freeze drift types and trace their origin rather than mislabel them. I want to inspect struct_map.json for e5341003's merge history, evidence, and bodies to see if it traces back to a band type that got renamed or split—if so, the fold policy should apply. I'll write a scratch script to pull a few fields from the top new types and check whether their sites' bodies reference canonical types from the latest rung.

I'm wondering why these new hash types emerged after the freeze, and whether they trace back to canonical-covered sites in the post-T2 census.

One possibility is that T4's respelling of sites into members left remnant raw sites on a base that lost their union-find link, spawning fresh hashes as drift from existing band types. The T3 E1 detection logic and the residue_cause fallback to the body's latest canonical draw should have caught this, but maybe it didn't fire because the base in question wasn't the one drawn by that row — meaning this isn't just a cleanup sweep but a real decision point on a batch of new types.

I'm weighing whether to extend the tool with a new decision mode for post-freeze drift types — folding E1 matches to drawn canonical types, falling back to residual-by-layout-class, or defining new types outright — but that's a heavier change than this phase's scope, since defining types is supposed to be T3's job. Given the scale involved, I should actually measure how many distinct new types there are among the 7,128 overlay sites, whether their lineage is clearly E1, and their size distribution, since large types spanning many bodies would clearly need formal definitions. Given the ambiguity, flagging this as a question with a recommendation seems like the right move rather than trying to force a decision inside this sweep.

I should check whether the census file is tracked in git and look at its state at the T3 commit, checking if that particular struct type was already present before T4.

---
ts: 2026-10-16T11:45:56.071Z

---

question. The T4 overlay sweep is finished, but 7,128 overlay sites still read TYPE-NOT-CANONICAL, so one done-when clause is not met. Those sites sit on struct names created by the sweep itself, and no rule decides them yet.

What is done: 24 restruct_cycle.sh batches (t4_S1..S24, last 1e4f36bacf), each final 100/100 identical, R22 218/218 and committed. The drawable set shrank every batch, 2,417 → 138 → 38 files, and t4_S25 hit the zero-work exit. No file was written with 0 conversions, and tail-only bodies were skipped by the planner.
- Sweep: 55,583 sites became member accesses. Kept casts are backed CAST_* macros, unbacked 0.
- Raw casts, all segments: P 174,987 → 120,360 and X 12,031 → 10,390.
- Residue chain on ov: `--residue --forms P,X,I,M`, census, `--restamp` (56,625 sites restamped), then the NO-EDIT variant once (7,542 sites → TYPE-NO-MEMBER). NO-EDIT went 29,684 → 61,378 → 53,763.
- `--check-residue --only ov_*` reports uncovered 0, and `--check-casts --residue` exits 0 (both verify commands green).
- In ov, band types have 0 TNC sites and the tail reads TYPE-BELOW-BAND (5,009 sites). TNC fell from 139,520 to 23,630 overall; the md/main/shared share of that is T5's.

The open clause: the 7,128 ov sites span 652 map types in 1,820 bodies. None of them is in tnc_band.tsv or type_folds.tsv. They are new cluster hashes, formed when the census regrouped the raw sites the sweep left behind (e5341003, for example, did not exist at the T3 close). They are not small: 17 types hold 100 or more sites each, e5341003 alone 768 sites in 128 bodies. Relabelling them TYPE-BELOW-BAND would redefine that term, so I did not.

Decision needed: whether to amend T4's done-when and add a drift-decision task. I recommend yes.
- Add a task after T5, so the drift from all segments is decided in one pass and one header fan-out. It would add a `--decide-band --ranks drift` mode under the binding policy: E1 lineage first, then a residual layout class, then a define for types with 2 or more sites (the band cutoff), else TYPE-BELOW-BAND with evidence `drift:<sites>`. Then one header batch, a rung-S sweep of the touched bodies, and residue, restamp and check-residue on each segment.
- Amend T4's done-when to "band and tail ov sites off TNC; unranked drift types go to the new task" and close T4.

The per-batch figures, verdicts by cause and residual by cause are in the summary. Summary: /home/musashi/bfm-decomp/phase-ends/current/tasks/T4.md. Log: /home/musashi/bfm-decomp/phase-ends/current/logs/T4.md. Both committed as f4f13128e1.

**Project Architect 3.28.7 is installed and loaded. Ready to continue work.**

I answered T4's question myself, because it stays inside the milestone. T4 can now close.

**What T4 did.** It finished re-sweeping the overlays (the binaries loaded per scene or room) onto the new canonical struct types. That took 24 batches, every one byte-identical at 218/218 and committed. In all, 55,583 casts became plain struct member accesses. Raw pointer casts fell from about 175,000 to 120,000. Inside the 95% band, the overlays now have no "type not canonical" residue left. TNC (that "not canonical" label) fell from 139,520 to 23,630 sites overall, and the rest of that belongs to T5.

**What it hit.** 7,128 overlay sites still read TNC, spread over 652 struct types. These types aren't in the frozen band list or in the tail. They are new placeholder types, formed when the census regrouped the raw casts the sweep left behind. Some are big: one has 768 sites. The expert rightly declined to relabel them as "below band", because that would have stretched the term to make the check pass.

**What I decided:**
- **New task after T5.** It decides these leftover types for all the binaries in one pass, by the same rules as T3. First it tries tracing each one back to an existing type and merging it into that type. If that fails, it records a layout reason; otherwise it gives the type a proper definition. Then it runs one sweep of the bodies it touched.
- **No "below band" for leftovers.** Even single-site leftovers can't go there. The milestone caps that label at the roughly 7,000 tail sites frozen with the band, and the overlays alone already use 5,009 of them.
- **Asks before creating one-off structs.** If the task would create more than 500 single-site structs, it stops and asks first, because you didn't want structs that exist only to satisfy the counter.
- **T4 and T5 amended.** Their "no TNC left" check now covers the band and tail types only. The new task owns the check across all binaries, so the milestone itself doesn't change.

These changes go in at the next task boundary, so T5 runs as planned and then hands its leftovers to the new task.

T4's summary is in `/home/musashi/bfm-decomp/phase-ends/current/tasks/T4.md`.

The new expert works in this order:

3. **Finish T5.** Then it goes on to the shared-code sweep and the rest of T5, leaving the leftover types to T17.

The three committed batches all stay.

PA3 3.28.8 is out, and its main fix is the false "expert stopped" bug you saw on T2 and T5.

What changed:
- **No more false stops while an expert waits.** Experts and coders now hand back only once, when their work is done, so they stay on Claude Code's agent list while they wait on a coder. If an expert drops off the list while its coder is still working, the mod now treats it as waiting, not stopped. And if a stop was only guessed from a missing expert, that expert's own report clears it and the next task starts. That is exactly the loop from this morning.
- **Pictures reach me.** A picture you drag into the terminal or paste as a file path now gets passed to me, and I open it. A screenshot pasted straight from the clipboard still has no file, so I still see text only for those.
- **Fuller agent cards.** A closed expert or coder card now shows the report's text, not just its "Summary: <path>" line.
- **Keep-warm by role.** A "keep-warm" ping is a small message the mod sends a waiting agent so its cached context doesn't expire. Coders now get one about every 4.5 minutes while they wait on their own background job, for up to 2.5 hours; before, coders got none. Experts are kept warm for up to 12 hours, or 7 hours once their context passes 250k. I'm kept warm for between 2 and 12 hours, depending on how large my context is. Past 175k, a fresh Claude takes your next message.

**Project Architect 3.28.8 is installed and loaded. Ready to continue work.**

This update also changed a setting that only takes effect after a restart, so please restart Claude Code when convenient: /exit, then `claude --continue`.

Nothing has changed with the work. T5 is still paused, its tree is clean and its last commit is e033c139c5. When you're back and want to go on, tell me and I'll resume T5. With this fix, its expert should be able to wait on its coder without the false stops.

I'm questioning whether that pinned register is actually a missing parameter or just the compiler using it as scratch space in a void function, since the classifier's "missing-parameter" guess might be wrong here.

So the real test should be whether the pin is read before being written — that signals an incoming parameter value — versus written first, which just means it's a scratch register and adding a parameter wouldn't make sense since it'd break caller byte compatibility. This splits the 270 cases into true missing-parameter rewrites versus ones that should be marked as refusals since the pin is just scratch, not an incoming value.

I could sketch a quick scanner myself to classify each residue row by checking whether the pinned variable's first use is a read or a write, but given the scale I should delegate the heavier driver work — rewriting signatures and caller sites — to coders while I wait for the retriever, maybe just prototyping the classifier logic in my scratch directory.
